--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The mission is AGGREGATION - robots need to gather in the BLUE-walled strip (black floor). The score is minimized by having robots IN the target region (black floor strip near blue walls).

**Key insight:** The target region has BLACK floor and BLUE walls. The champion uses `GoAwayColor` (B=9) with color 2 (green) - moving away from green walls effectively pushes robots toward the blue strip. This makes sense!

**Analysis:**
- Best: GoAwayColor from green (clr=2) → score 14179
- 3rd: GoToColor toward color 1 (red?) → score 15848
- The champion is a simple 1-state FSM that just avoids green walls

**Color reference:** clr=1=red, clr=2=green, clr=3=blue, clr=4=yellow, clr=5=magenta, clr=6=cyan

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion (GoAwayColor green)
2. Try GoToColor blue (clr=3) - directly go to blue walls
3. Combine: GoAwayColor green + GoToColor blue
4. Add floor-color-based switching: when on gray floor, go toward blue; when on black floor, stop or aggregate
5. Try multi-state FSMs that use black floor detection to stay in target

**State design for multi-state:**
- State A: GoAwayColor(green) to navigate toward blue strip
- State B: Stop or Exploration when on black floor (stay in target)
- Transition: if floor is BLACK → switch to stop/stay behavior

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **Champion (exact copy)**
2. **GoToColor blue** - directly approach blue walls
3. **GoAwayColor green + GoToColor blue** 2-state, switch on floor color
4. **2-state: GoAwayColor(green) + Stop** - stop when on black floor
5. **GoAwayColor green with floor switching** 
6. **3-state FSM** with navigation and aggregation
7-14: Various explorations and exploitations

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion is `GoToColor blue (clr=3)` with score 3196.66 - dramatically better than the previous champion. This makes perfect sense: blue walls border the target strip, so moving toward blue walls brings robots into the black-floor target region.

**Key insight:** GoToColor(blue) is excellent. The target strip has blue walls, so robots that approach blue walls end up in the target region.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exact copy
2. Exploit: 2-state FSMs where robots go toward blue, then stop/stay when on black floor
3. Exploit: GoToColor(blue) + floor detection to stay in target
4. Explore: 3-state with go-to-blue, then aggregate (attraction) once in black region, then stay
5. The 2nd best (score 4732) uses GoAwayColor(green) → GoToColor(blue) when on gray floor - good idea but simpler is better

**Core logic:**
- State "navigate": GoToColor(blue) - approach target
- State "stay": Stop or slow exploration when on black floor (already in target)
- Transition: black floor detected → switch to stay behavior
- Transition back: gray floor detected → return to navigation

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion is still `GoToColor(blue)` with 1 state, score 3106.66. The 2-state variants that add a "slow exploration on black floor" do reasonably well (4590) but not as good as pure GoToColor(blue).

**Key observations:**
- Pure GoToColor(blue) is best - robots constantly seek blue walls = target region
- When on black floor (target), stopping or slow exploration is worse than continuing to seek blue
- GoAwayColor(green) → GoToColor(blue) is decent (4831) 
- The pattern "GoToColor(blue) on gray/white, slow wander on black" (4590) is 2nd best

**Why GoToColor(blue) works so well alone:** Blue walls are only around the target strip, so all robots perpetually face/approach blue, keeping them in the target region.

**Can we improve?** The score is ~3106 which means ~2.6 robots outside on average (3106/1200 ≈ 2.6). Very good already.

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. 2-state: GoToColor(blue) + when on black floor, very slow wander (low rwm) to avoid pushing neighbors out
2. 2-state: GoToColor(blue) + Stop on black floor (pure stop, very high probability)
3. 2-state: GoToColor(blue) + Attraction on black floor (aggregate with neighbors in target)
4. Try different probabilities for the black-floor transition
5. 3-state with GoToColor(blue) → Stop on black → Attraction when many neighbors

The 2nd best uses rwm=10 (very slow turning) on black floor with p=0.8 for the transition. Let me try variations:
- Higher/lower probabilities for the black-floor transition
- Different behaviors on black floor
- Keep GoToColor(blue) as dominant behavior

- 2nd best pattern: GoToColor(blue) → on black floor → slow exploration (rwm=10-20), transition back on gray
- Stop on black floor is WORSE than slow exploration (rwm works, Stop doesn't)
- 3-state with GoToColor→slow wander→attraction is decent (4675)

**Why GoToColor(blue) alone wins:** Robots perpetually approach blue walls, staying in the target black-floor strip. Adding a secondary behavior risks robots wandering out.

**The gap:** Champion ~3106, 2nd best ~4529. The 2-state variants are ~45% worse. The single state is likely better because multi-state robots sometimes oscillate or leave the target.

**Exploitation focus:**
- Keep champion
- Try variations of the 2-state GoToColor(blue)+slow_wander with different probabilities (0.7, 0.75, 0.85) and rwm values (5, 15, 25, 30)
- Try very high probability transitions (0.95, 0.99) to minimize time in secondary state
- Try 2-state with rwm=1 (almost stopped but still moving)

**Exploration:**
- Try GoToColor(blue) with neighbor-based transitions: if many neighbors (already aggregated), switch to slow wander
- Try ZigZag toward blue (not possible directly, but zigzag then switch)
- Try FlockCenter + GoToColor(blue) hybrid

The pattern "GoToColor(blue) → [black floor, p=0.8] → slow wander (rwm=20) → [gray, p=0.9] → GoToColor(blue)" is best 2-state. Let me tune rwm and probabilities more carefully.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Current state of knowledge:**
- Champion: 1-state GoToColor(blue) = 3106.66 (consistently best)
- Best 2-state: GoToColor(blue) + slow wander (rwm=20-30) on black floor, with extra random transition (p=0.02) back ≈ 4264
- 3-state with FlockCenter on black floor ≈ 4354
- Stop on black floor is WORSE than slow wander
- rwm=5 is worse than rwm=20-30
- Neighbor-based transitions don't help much

**The champion is hard to beat.** The 1-state solution is optimal in its simplicity. The score of ~3106 means about 2.6 robots outside on average over 1200 steps - very good.

**Can we beat 3106?** The 1-state GoToColor(blue) keeps robots near blue walls. The issue is that robots might overshoot and exit the other side, or robots starting far away take time to arrive.

**New ideas to try:**
1. The champion uses `--cle0 5` (magenta LED). Does LED color matter? Probably not for scoring.
2. What if we add a very small probability of switching to something that helps robots already in the target stay there? The problem is GoToColor(blue) might push robots INTO the wall, causing bouncing.
3. Try GoToColor(blue) with a neighbor-based escape: if many neighbors detected, switch to slow wander briefly
4. Try 2-state: GoToColor(blue) → [many neighbors, high threshold] → slow wander → [few neighbors] → GoToColor(blue). This might help pack robots tighter.
5. Try FlockCenter as primary with GoToColor(blue) as secondary
6. Try ZigZag as exploration toward blue

**Key insight I haven't tried:** What if I add a transition from GoToColor(blue) based on seeing many neighbors (already aggregated) → switch to attraction or stop? The 3-state with FlockCenter did well (4354).

**Best exploitation:** The 2nd best (4264) uses GoToColor(blue) + rwm=20 + extra random transition p=0.02. Let me vary the extra transition probability and the rwm.

**Also:** Try the 3-state variant more carefully - the one with FlockCenter scored 4354. Try with different fc values and thresholds.

## Reasoning

**Current state:**
- Champion: 1-state GoToColor(blue) = 3106.66 (unchanged for 4 rounds)
- Best 2-state this round: ~3994 (rwm=20, p_black=0.8, p_random=0.05)
- Pattern is clear: GoToColor(blue) → black floor → slow wander → gray floor → back

**Key observations:**
- rwm=20-25 on black floor is better than rwm=5 or rwm=30
- p_black=0.75-0.8 is better than 0.85-0.9 (don't switch too eagerly)
- Adding a small random back-transition (p=0.02-0.05) helps slightly
- 3-state and 4-state machines are generally worse
- The champion 1-state is consistently best

**Why can't we beat the champion?** The 1-state GoToColor(blue) is elegant: robots always face blue walls (target), minimizing time outside. Any secondary behavior risks robots drifting away.

**New ideas:**
1. Try p_black=0.7 with rwm=20 and p_random=0.05 (lower entry threshold into wander)
2. Try p_black=0.8, rwm=22, p_random=0.03 (fine-tuning)
3. Try p_gray=0.85 (different return threshold)  
4. Try 2-state with GoToColor(blue) → black floor → GoToColor(blue) again with attraction mixed in
5. Try adding a neighbor-count transition from GoToColor(blue): when many neighbors (already in group), briefly switch to slow wander to avoid pushing
6. Exploration: Try ZigZag + GoToColor(blue) combo
7. Try GoToColor(blue) with LED=0 (different LED, shouldn't matter but worth trying)

The best 2-state is about 28% worse than champion. I should focus more on the champion neighborhood and do more exploration.

**Exploration ideas:**
- What if the black floor wander has a very low rwm (1-3) making it nearly straight-line?
- What about using Spiral in black region?
- Try GoToColor with clr=3 (blue) but different transition conditions

## Reasoning

**Current state:**
- Champion: 1-state GoToColor(blue) = 3106.66 (unchanged for 6 rounds)
- Best 2-state this round: ~3935 with dual transitions from GoToColor: black floor (p=0.8) AND many neighbors (C=3, p=6-8, w=5-8) → slow wander (rwm=20) → gray floor (p=0.9) + random (p=0.05)
- The neighbor-count transition added to GoToColor state is helping the 2-state machines improve (3935 vs 3994 previously)

**Key insight:** Adding a neighbor-count trigger (C=3, threshold=6-8) to the GoToColor state helps. When many robots are already gathered, GoToColor robots stop pushing and switch to gentle wander. This makes sense physically.

**Exploitation focus:**
1. Fine-tune the neighbor threshold (try 5, 7, 9, 10) and steepness (4, 6, 10, 15)
2. Fine-tune rwm in wander state (18, 20, 22, 25)
3. Try combining both black-floor AND neighbor-count transitions from GoToColor
4. Try p_gray=0.85 vs 0.9 for return transition
5. Try p_random=0.03-0.08 for the random back-transition

**Why champion still wins:** The 1-state GoToColor(blue) is essentially perfect - robots always face blue walls. Any secondary state risks some robots spending time not approaching blue. The ~26% gap between champion and 2nd best may be irreducible with this FSM structure.

**Exploration:**
- Try 3-state: GoToColor(blue) → [black floor] → slow wander → [many neighbors] → attraction → [few neighbors] → back to wander
- Try adding the neighbor-count transition with lower threshold (3-4) to catch early aggregation

## Reasoning

**Current state:**
- Champion: 1-state GoToColor(blue) = 3106.66 (7 rounds unchanged)
- Best 2-state this round: 3416 - with dual transitions from GoToColor: black floor (p=0.8) AND many neighbors (C=3, threshold=6, w=8) → slow wander (rwm=20) → gray (p=0.9) + random (p=0.05) + few neighbors (C=4, threshold=2, w=10)
- The 3-transition wander state (gray + random + few-neighbors) is the best 2-state found so far

**Key findings:**
- p_black=0.8 consistently best (0.75 is worse)
- threshold=6-9 with various steepness all similar (~3650-3686)
- threshold=4, w=12 is close (3686)
- 3-state machines worse than 2-state
- Adding random 3rd transition to GoToColor state hurts (3874)
- The wander state with 3 transitions (gray + random + few-neighbors) scores 3416 - best 2-state ever!

**Exploitation of best 2-state (3416):**
- Vary the neighbor threshold (5, 7) and steepness (6, 10, 15)
- Vary the few-neighbor return threshold (1, 3) and steepness
- Vary rwm (18, 22)
- Vary p_random (0.03, 0.07)

**Exploration:**
- Try adding the 3rd wander transition (few-neighbors) to previously good 2-state topologies with different neighbor thresholds
- Try 2-state where wander also has GoToColor(blue) as a conditional return

The best 2-state ever has wander with: gray→back, random(0.05)→back, few-neighbors(2,10)→back. This makes sense: robots leave wander when floor turns gray (drifted out), randomly (to re-approach blue), or when cluster disperses.

## Reasoning

**Final round - maximize exploitation of best patterns:**

**Champion (3106.66):** 1-state GoToColor(blue) - unchanged for 9 rounds. This is likely the global optimum for this structure.

**Best 2-state pattern (3434):** 
- State 0: GoToColor(blue) with 2 transitions: black floor (p=0.8) OR many neighbors (C=3, threshold=6, w=8)
- State 1: slow wander (rwm=18) with 3 transitions: gray floor (p=0.9) + random (p=0.05) + few neighbors (C=4, threshold=2, w=10)
- rwm=18 slightly better than 20 or 22

**Key parameter ranges established:**
- p_black=0.8 (fixed)
- neighbor threshold=6, w=8 for GoToColor exit (best)
- rwm=16-20 for wander (rwm=18 best)
- p_gray=0.9 for wander exit (fixed)
- p_random=0.05 for wander exit (0.03 and 0.07 worse)
- few-neighbor: threshold=2, w=10 for wander exit

**Final round strategy:**
1. Champion exact copy
2. Multiple copies of best 2-state with very fine-tuned variations
3. Try rwm=16, 17, 19 around the sweet spot of 18
4. Try few-neighbor threshold=1 with high steepness (robots leave wander only when very alone)
5. Try p_gray=0.88, 0.92 variations
6. Try neighbor exit threshold=6 with w=7, 9, 10
7. One exploration: 3-state with the best 2-state topology extended

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 16 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.34 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
